In what follows, we denote by * the index of the marked point fixed at
\infty
.
Consider an arbitrary stable curve
(C;x_{\bullet})\in\overline{W_{\tau}}
. For
i=0,1,2,3
, let

(C_{i};x^{(i)}_{\bullet})

denote its projection onto
\overline{W_{\tau_{i}}}
under the homeomorphism given in Proposition
3.11
.
The curve
(C;x_{\bullet})
(with
x_{*}=\infty
on the component containing it) admits one of two possible tree structures:

(1)

C=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
is smooth. Then for some
\sigma\in S_{7}
fixing the sets
\{1,2,3\},\{4,5\},\{6,7\}
, we have
x_{\sigma(1)}\leq\ldots\leq x_{\sigma(7)}
. Setting
d_{i}=x_{\sigma(i+1)}-x_{\sigma(i)}
, we have
\max\{d_{1},d_{2}\}\leq d_{3}
, and
\max\{d_{4},d_{6}\}\leq d_{5}\leq d_{3}
. (See Figure
9
left.) Note that
d_{3}>0
due to stability and that
d_{5}>0
since the the marked points
x_{4},x_{5},x_{6},x_{7}
cannot collide without bubbling off. For
i=0,1,2,3
, we have
C_{i}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
with

\displaystyle x^{(0)}_{\bullet}

\displaystyle=(x^{(0)}_{1},x^{(0)}_{2},x^{(0)}_{3},x^{(0)}_{*})

\displaystyle=(0,d_{3},d_{3}+d_{5},\infty)

\displaystyle x^{(1)}_{\bullet}

\displaystyle=(x^{(1)}_{\sigma(1)},x^{(1)}_{\sigma(2)},x^{(1)}_{\sigma(3)},x^{(1)}_{\hbox to3.41pt{\vbox to3.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.70694pt\lower-1.70694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.09 0 C 2.09 1.15 1.15 2.09 0 2.09 C -1.15 2.09 -2.09 1.15 -2.09 0 C -2.09 -1.15 -1.15 -2.09 0 -2.09 C 1.15 -2.09 2.09 -1.15 2.09 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}},x^{(1)}_{*})\

\displaystyle=(0,d_{1},d_{1}+d_{2},d_{1}+d_{2}+d_{3},\infty)

\displaystyle x^{(2)}_{\bullet}

\displaystyle=(x^{(2)}_{\sigma(4)},x^{(2)}_{\sigma(5)},x^{(2)}_{\hbox to3.41pt{\vbox to3.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.70694pt\lower-1.70694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.09 0 C 2.09 1.15 1.15 2.09 0 2.09 C -1.15 2.09 -2.09 1.15 -2.09 0 C -2.09 -1.15 -1.15 -2.09 0 -2.09 C 1.15 -2.09 2.09 -1.15 2.09 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\displaystyle=(x^{(3)}_{\hbox to3.41pt{\vbox to3.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.70694pt\lower-1.70694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.09 0 C 2.09 1.15 1.15 2.09 0 2.09 C -1.15 2.09 -2.09 1.15 -2.09 0 C -2.09 -1.15 -1.15 -2.09 0 -2.09 C 1.15 -2.09 2.09 -1.15 2.09 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\displaystyle=(-d_{5},0,d_{6},\infty).

(2)

C
consists of two components,
C^{*}\cong\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
containing
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{*}
; and
C^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
containing

. Let

be the node of
C
, and set

on

. For some
\sigma\in S_{7}
fixing the sets
\{1,2,3\},\{4,5\},\{6,7\}

9

•

on
C^{*}
:
d_{1}=x_{\sigma(2)}-x_{\sigma(1)}\geq 0
,

,
d_{3}=y-x_{\sigma(3)}>0
with
\max\{d_{1},d_{2}\}\leq d_{3}
;

•

on
C^{\prime}
:
d_{i}=x_{\sigma(i+1)}-x_{\sigma(i)}\geq 0
for

with
\max\{d_{4},d_{6}\}\leq d_{5}
and
d_{5}>0
.

,
C_{i}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
with

\displaystyle=(0,d_{1},d_{1}+d_{2},d_{1}+d_{2}+d_{3},\infty)

\displaystyle x^{(2)}_{\bullet}

\displaystyle=(x^{(2)}_{\sigma(4)},x^{(2)}_{\sigma(5)},x^{(2)}_{\hbox to3.41pt{\vbox to3.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.70694pt\lower-1.70694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.09 0 C 2.09 1.15 1.15 2.09 0 2.09 C -1.15 2.09 -2.09 1.15 -2.09 0 C -2.09 -1.15 -1.15 -2.09 0 -2.09 C 1.15 -2.09 2.09 -1.15 2.09 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\displaystyle=(0,d_{3},\infty);

\displaystyle\text{ on }C_{0}^{\prime}:\

\displaystyle(x^{(0)}_{2},x^{(0)}_{3},y^{(0)})

\displaystyle=(0,d_{5},\infty).

Note that
(C^{(0)},x^{(0)}_{\bullet})
is the unique element in
\overline{W_{\tau_{0}}}
that corresponds to a singular curve.

Remark 10
.

With notation as in Proposition
3.11
, we have
\overline{W_{\tau_{0}}}\cong[-1,1]^{E_{\Int}(\tau_{0})}
by Theorem
3.8
from the unweighted case. Thus, in order to show that our weighted dual cells
\overline{W_{\tau}}
are indeed cells, it remains and is sufficient to consider the case
a=n-1
, with
\tau
the unique
(n-1)
-stable tree on
[n]
with one leaf labeled
[n-1]
and the other labeled
\{n\}
, both attached to the root. We will see that in this case,
\overline{W_{\tau}}
is naturally identified with the permutahedron.

3.2.3.
Maps between products of permutahedra and Hassett spaces

The
n
-th order
permutahedron

\Pi_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the convex hull

(26)

\Pi_{n}:=\mathrm{conv}\{\vec{\sigma}:=(\sigma(1),\sigma(2),\ldots,\sigma(n))\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\sigma\in S_{n}\}.

Note that
\Pi_{n}
is an
(n-1)
-dimensional polytope contained in the hyperplane

\left\{\sum x_{i}=\frac{n(n+1)}{2}\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Faces of
\Pi_{n}
correspond to compositions of
[n]
. Let
A_{\bullet}=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{r})
be a composition of
[n]
, let
\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}
be the corresponding face and
\vec{o}_{A_{\bullet}}
its centroid. Then
\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}
has dimension
n-r
and is the convex hull of the points
\vec{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
satisfying

\displaystyle\{x_{i}:i\in A_{1}\}

\displaystyle=\{1,2,\ldots,|A_{1}|\};

\displaystyle\{x_{i}:i\in A_{1}\cup A_{2}\}

\displaystyle=\{1,2,\ldots,|A_{1}|+|A_{2}|\};

\displaystyle\{x_{i}:i\in A_{1}\cup\ldots\cup A_{r-1}\}

\displaystyle=\{1,2,\ldots,|A_{1}|+\ldots+|A_{r-1}|\};

\displaystyle\{x_{i}:i\in[n]\}

\displaystyle=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}.

That is,
\vec{x}=\vec{\sigma}
for some
\sigma\in S_{n}
where
{\sigma}^{-1}
is compatible with
A_{\bullet}
. It is straightforward to see that
\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}
is combinatorially equivalent to the product of permutahedra
\Pi_{|A_{1}|}\times\Pi_{|A_{2}|}\times\ldots\times\Pi_{|A_{r}|}
. We have
\Pi_{B_{\bullet}}\subseteq\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}
if and only if
B_{\bullet}
is a refinement of
A_{\bullet}
; in particular

\partial\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}=\bigcup_{B_{\bullet}}\Pi_{B_{\bullet}}=\coprod_{B_{\bullet}}\Int(\Pi_{B_{\bullet}}),

where
B_{\bullet}
ranges over all proper refinements of
A_{\bullet}
.

Let
a=n-1
, and fix
\tau
to be the tree

\tau=\vbox{\hbox{

}}.

Note that, by the
(n-1)
-stability condition, every
(C;x_{\bullet})\in\overline{M_{0,{\mathcal{A}({n-1})}}}
is a smooth curve, in particular irreducible. For the remainder of this section, we assume that the
(n+1)
-st marked point is fixed at
\infty
and not explicitly mention this fact in our discussion. The closed cell
\overline{W_{\tau}}\subseteq\overline{M_{0,{\mathcal{A}({n-1})}}}(\mathbb{R})
consists of the all the curves of the form
(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1};x_{\bullet})
such that, for some
\sigma\in S_{n}
with
\sigma(n)=n
,

x_{\sigma(1)}\leq x_{\sigma(2)}\leq\ldots\leq x_{\sigma(n)}=x_{n}

with

\max_{i\in[n-2]}(x_{\sigma(i+1)}-x_{\sigma(i)})\leq x_{\sigma(n)}-x_{\sigma(n-1)}=1.

Intuitively, the marked points indexed at
1,\ldots,n-1
are close together in the sense that they have “small consecutive gaps”; whereas the
n
-th marked point is “far away” from the first
n-1
marked points.

We describe a particular map
\varphi:\Pi_{n-1}\to\overline{W_{\tau}}
and show that it is a homeomorphism. We identify
S_{n-1}\subseteq S_{n}
as the permutations for which
\sigma(n)=n
,

and we define
\varphi
inductively by dimension on the faces of
\Pi_{n-1}
, such that the face
\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}
corresponds to the curves for which the following holds: rescaling so that
d_{n-1}=1
,

(27)

\displaystyle d_{i}=1\text{ if and only if }i=n-1\text{ or }\sigma(i),\sigma(i+1)\text{ are in different parts of }A_{\bullet}.

For
\sigma\in S_{n-1}\subseteq S_{n}
, define

\varphi(\vec{\sigma}):=(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1};\sigma(1),\sigma(2),\ldots,\sigma(n-1),\sigma(n)=n,\infty)

to be the permutation point corresponding to
{\sigma}^{-1}
. This defines
\varphi
on the vertices (0-dimensional faces) of
\Pi_{n-1}
and satisfies (
27
). Next, fix a composition
A_{\bullet}=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{r})
of
[n-1]
where
r<n
. Assume
\varphi
is defined compatibly with (
27
) and continuously on the union of the faces of
\Pi_{n-1}
of dimension less than
\dim\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}=n-r
. In particular,
\varphi|_{\partial\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}}
is completely determined. Every
\vec{z}\in\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}
is then uniquely expressible as

\vec{z}=t\vec{y}+(1-t)\vec{o}_{A_{\bullet}}

for some
\vec{y}\in\partial\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}
and some
t
, and where where
\vec{o}_{A_{\bullet}}
denotes the centroid of the face. We have
\varphi(\vec{y})=(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1};x_{\bullet})
, where for some
\sigma\in S_{n-1}\subseteq S_{n}
consistent with
A_{\bullet}
, we have

(28)

x_{\sigma(1)}\leq x_{\sigma(2)}\leq\ldots\leq x_{\sigma(n-1)}<x_{\sigma(n)}=x_{n}.

We set
d_{i}:=x_{\sigma(i+1)}-x_{\sigma(i)}
for all
i
. Up to translation and scaling, we may assume
x_{\sigma(1)}=0
and that
\max_{i}(d_{i})=1
. In particular,
d_{i}\leq 1
for all
i
, and by (
27
),
d_{i}=1
if
i=n-1
or
\sigma(i),\sigma(i+1)
lie in different parts of
A_{\bullet}
.

We define
\varphi(\vec{z})
by scaling by
t
the successive distances internal to each part of
A_{\bullet}
, putting

(29)

d^{\prime}_{i}:=\begin{cases}td_{i},&\sigma(i),\sigma(i+1)\text{ lie in the same part of }A_{\bullet}\\
d_{i}=1,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

We define
\varphi(\vec{z})
to be the stable curve
(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1};x_{\bullet})
where

x_{\sigma(i)}=\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}d^{\prime}_{j}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq n,

considering the empty sum to be
0
.

Remark 11
.

The map
\varphi
is well defined.

(1)

Our construction of
\varphi(\vec{z})
from
\varphi(\vec{y})
is independent of the choice of
\sigma
.

(2)

(
t=0
case) If
A_{\bullet}=(A_{1}\ldots,A_{r})
is a composition of
[n-1]
, then
\varphi(\vec{o}_{A_{\bullet}})
is the stable curve that sets the
i
-th marked point at
j-1
if
i\in A_{j}
and the
n
-th marked point at
r
. In this case each
d^{\prime}_{i}\in\{0,1\}
. For example, if
A_{\bullet}=(\{1,3\},\{2,5,6\},\{4\},\{7,8\})
, then

\varphi(\vec{o}_{A_{\bullet}})=\vbox{\hbox{

}}\in\overline{M_{0,{\mathcal{A}({n-1})}}}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{7}.

(3)

(
t=1
case) If
\vec{z}=\vec{y}
, then indeed, our definitions of
\varphi(\vec{z})
and
\varphi(\vec{y})
coincide since
d^{\prime}_{i}=d_{i}
.

By (
2
),
\varphi
is continuous at
\vec{o}_{A_{\bullet}}
and hence on all of
\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}
, and compatible with (
27
). Taking
A_{\bullet}=([n-1])
to be the trivial composition implies that
\varphi
is continuous.

We briefly describe the inverse map
\theta:\overline{W_{\tau}}\to\Pi_{n-1}
. Given a stable curve
C=(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1};x_{\bullet})
from
\overline{W_{\tau}}
, choose
\sigma
to satisfy (
28
), define
d_{i}:=x_{\sigma(i+1)}-x_{\sigma(i)}
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
, and normalize so that
\max d_{i}=1
. The vector
\vec{d}=(d_{i})
of distances of consecutively placed marked points induces a composition
A_{\bullet}
of
[n-1]
compatible with
\sigma
, satisfying (
27
).

As above, we define
\theta
inductively according to
\dim\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}
. If every
d_{i}=1
, then
C=\varphi({\vec{\sigma}}^{-1})
. Suppose at least one of the
d_{i}
is strictly less than
1
and define

t:=\max_{d_{i}<1}d_{i}.

If
t=0
, then
C=\varphi(\vec{o}_{A_{\bullet}})
. Assume
t\in(0,1)
. Consider the stable curve
C^{\prime}=(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1};x^{\prime}_{\bullet})
, where
x^{\prime}_{1}=0
,
x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{\sigma(n-1)}=1
, and for
2\leq i\leq n-1
,

x^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)}-x^{\prime}_{\sigma(i-1)}=\min\left\{1,\frac{d_{i-1}}{t}\right\}.

By the inductive hypothesis,
C^{\prime}=\varphi(\vec{y})
for some
\vec{y}\in\Pi_{n-1}
contained in a face of dimension smaller than
\dim\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}
. By construction,
\vec{y}\in\partial\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}
. It readily follows that
C=\varphi(t\vec{o}_{A_{\bullet}}+(1-t)\vec{y})
, showing that
\varphi
is a bijection. By compactness of the Hausdorff spaces
\Pi_{n-1}
and
\overline{W_{\tau}}
,
\varphi
is a homeomorphism. Our discussion proves the following result.

.

and let

There is a homeomorphism
\Pi_{n-1}\cong\overline{W_{\tau}}
, taking the face
\Pi_{A_{\bullet}}
, for each composition
A_{\bullet}
of
[n]
, to the set of curves satisfying (
27
).
In particular,
W_{\tau}=\Int(\overline{W_{\tau}})
is homeomorphic to the ball of dimension
n-2
.

Remark 12

(Star-shaped polytopes)
.

With
n=4
, the cell
W_{\tau}
of Theorem
3.13
uses the conditions

\max_{i=1,2}(x_{\sigma(i+1)}-x_{\sigma(i)})\leq x_{\sigma(4)}-x_{\sigma(3)},

where
\sigma\in S_{4}
is the permutation such that

x_{\sigma(1)}\leq x_{\sigma(2)}\leq x_{\sigma(3)}\leq x_{\sigma(4)},

and it is assumed that
\sigma(4)=4
. This condition is in fact only star-convex around the origin, not convex. If we rescale so that
x_{4}-x_{\sigma(3)}=1
and translate so that
x_{\sigma(1)}=0
, then
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is a permutation of
(0,d_{1},d_{1}+d_{2})
where
\max(d_{1},d_{2})\leq 1
. The six resulting regions form a star-shaped two-dimensional subset of the coordinate planes of the first octant of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
:

This is why the homeomorphism
\varphi
to the permutahedron is likewise defined in a star-shaped manner. The star polytope, and certain quotients of it, also arise in
[
Ili+23
]
, e.g. see
[
Ili+23
, Theorem 8.2]
.

}}\in\Rtree([n];a).

The same argument as in Theorem
3.13
directly yields a homeomorphism

\overline{W_{\tau}}\cong\prod_{i=1}^{r}\Pi_{A_{i}}\cong\Pi_{A_{\bullet}},

which recovers the product decomposition of Proposition
3.11
for this
\tau
.

Corollary 3.14
.

Let
A_{\bullet}=(A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{r})
be a composition of
[n]
. Let
\tau\in\Rtree(A_{\bullet})
be
a
-stable. Then

\overline{W_{\tau}}\cong[-1,1]^{|E_{\Int}(\tau)|}\times\Pi_{|A_{1}|}\times\ldots\times\Pi_{|A_{r}|}.

In particular,
W_{\tau}
is a cell of dimension
|E_{\Int}(\tau)|+n-r
.

Proof.

The result follows immediately from Proposition
3.11
and Theorem
3.13
.
∎

Remark 14
.

(1)

If
\tau
is a binary tree, then it has exactly
r-2
internal edges, so
\dim W_{\tau}=\dim\overline{W_{\tau}}=n-2
, which is equal to the dimension of the Hassett space
\overline{M_{0,\mathcal{A}(a)}}(\mathbb{R})
.

(2)

In general,
\codim W_{\tau}=\sum_{v\in V_{\Int}(\tau)}(c(v)-2)
, where
c(v)
is the number of children of the vertex
v
. This coincides with the formula given in Equation (
17
).

Corollary 3.15
.

The cells
W_{\tau}
form a cell decomposition of
\overline{M_{0,\mathcal{A}(a)}}(\mathbb{R})
:

\overline{M_{0,\mathcal{A}(a)}}(\mathbb{R})=\coprod_{\tau\in\StRtree([n];a)}W_{\tau}.

As discussed in Remark
9
, for each
\tau\in\StRtree([n];a)
, the cell
W_{\tau}
is the image of several cells under the reduction map from
\overline{M_{0,{n+1}}}(\mathbb{R})
or from
\overline{M_{0,{\mathcal{A}({a-1})}}}(\mathbb{R})
. This decomposition, highlighting the structure of
W_{\tau}
in terms of permutahedra, can be seen in Figure
1
for
\Pi_{3}
and Figure
10
for
\Pi_{4}
.

Figure 10
.
With
n=5
, there are three-dimensional permutahedra
\overline{W_{\tau}}\cong\Pi_{4}
in
\overline{M_{0,{\mathcal{A}(4)}}}(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{3}
. Such a cell is the image under the reduction map of seven polytopal cells
\overline{W_{\tau^{\prime}}}\subseteq\overline{M_{0,{\mathcal{A}(3)}}}(\mathbb{R})
, shown above. Each antipodal pair of polytopes represents a single cell
\overline{W_{\tau^{\prime}}}
, by gluing the inner two faces via the antipodal map. Thus, there are four copies of
[-1,1]\times\Pi_{3}
(hexagonal prisms, in orange) and three copies of
[-1,1]\times\Pi_{2}\times\Pi_{2}
(cubes, in blue). The inner boundary is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
(the exceptional divisor) and the reduction map contracts it to a point to yield
\overline{W_{\tau}}\cong\Pi_{4}
. Note that each hexagonal prism is itself the image of three cubes from
\overline{M_{0,{n+1}}}(\mathbb{R})
; that decomposition resembles Figure
1
(crossed with
\Pi_{2}
).

Remark 15
.

For an arbitrary vector of weights
\mathcal{A}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{n+1}=1)
, there is an analogous procedure for compressing rooted trees from
\Rtree([n])
to render them
\mathcal{A}
-stable. Likewise, there is an analogous
\mathcal{A}
-weighted distance algorithm, given by running the standard distance algorithm and compressing the result to an
\mathcal{A}
-stable tree. The results of this section then carry through to show that the
\mathcal{A}
-stable trees index a dual cell decomposition of
\overline{M_{0,\mathcal{A}}}(\mathbb{R})
by products of permutahedra and intervals. Indeed, similar remarks apply to the simplicially-stable moduli spaces of
[
BB24
]
containing an isolated vertex. However, the resulting decomposition is typically not
S_{n}
-symmetric, so we do not know a useful way to extract a description of
\pi_{1}(\overline{M_{0,\mathcal{A}}}(\mathbb{R}))
in general.

3.2.4.
The
2
-skeleton and presentation of the weighted cactus group

We now classify the dual cells
W_{\tau}
up to dimension two such that
\overline{W_{\tau}}
contains the permutation point
(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1};1,2,\ldots,n,\infty)
. If
\tau
has leaf labels
A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}
, this condition is equivalent to requiring that
A_{\bullet}
be compatible with the identity permutation.

•

(Dimension 0) The
0
-cells are precisely the permutation points. We note that the base point is itself a
0
-cell.

•

(Dimension 1) There are two types of 1-cell adjacent to the base point.

(1)

A_{\bullet}
has one part
\{i,i+1\}
of size
2
and all other parts singletons, and
\tau
has no internal edges. The other endpoint of
\overline{W_{\tau}}
is the permutation point corresponding to
w_{i,i+1}\in S_{n}
.

(2)

A_{\bullet}=(\{1\},\ldots,\{n\})
and
\tau
has one internal edge, whose subtree contains the leaves
\{p\},\ldots,\{q\}
for some
p<q
. By
a
-stability, we must have
q+1-p>a
. The other endpoint of
\overline{W_{\tau}}
is the permutation point corresponding to
w_{p,q}\in S_{n}
.

•

(Dimension 2) There are four kinds of
2
-cell adjacent to the base point. We list them according to their combinatorial types. See Figure
11
for representative examples.

(1)

(Hexagon,
\Pi_{3}
):
A_{\bullet}
has one part of size
3
,
\{i,i+1,i+2\}
for some
i
, and
\tau
has no internal edges.

(2)

(Square,
\Pi_{2}\times\Pi_{2}
):
A_{\bullet}
has two parts of size 2,
\{i,i+1\}
and
\{j,j+1\}
with
i+1<j
, and
\tau
has no internal edges.

(3)

(Square,
[-1,1]\times\Pi_{2}
):
A_{\bullet}
has one part of size
2
,
\{i,i+1\}
, and
\tau
has one internal edge, whose subtree contains the labels
p,\ldots,q
, which may or may not include the leaf labeled
\{i,i+1\}
. By
a
-stability, we must have
q+1-p>a
.

(4)

(Square,
[-1,1]^{2}
):
A_{\bullet}=(\{1\},\ldots,\{n\})
and
\tau
has two internal edges, which correspond to either nested or disjoint subintervals of leaves (similar to the unweighted case); see Figure
8
. By
a
-stability, the subintervals must have length
>a
.

.

a
-stable
2
-cells.

\Pi_{3}
(
a=3)
.

).

).

[-1,1]^{2}
(
a=2
).

Remark 16
.

As in the unweighted case, the cell decomposition of
\overline{M_{0,{\mathcal{A}({a})}}}(\mathbb{R})
lifts to the double cover
\overline{{M}^{2:1}_{0,\mathcal{A}({a})}}(\mathbb{R})
, with dual cells indexed by
a
-stable trees, but where the root vertex is never considered flippable. The preimage of the dual cell
W_{\tau}\subseteq\overline{{M}^{2:1}_{0,\mathcal{A}({a})}}(\mathbb{R})
is thus the disjoint union of two cells that differ by flipping at the root. The remaining details are essentially the same as in the unweighted case discussed in
3.1.4
, and will be omitted here.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Recall that the
S_{n}
-action on
\overline{{M}^{2:1}_{0,n+1}}(\mathbb{R})
is simply transitive on the dual
0
-cells. This action descends to an
S_{n}
-action on
\overline{{M}^{2:1}_{0,\mathcal{A}(a)}}(\mathbb{R})
simply transitive on its dual
0
-cells. Hence, an argument similar to the one given in
3.1.4
and Theorem
2.4
gives a presentation for
\tilde{J}_{n}^{a}
. Proposition
2.7
then yields the desired presentation of
J_{n}^{a}
.

It remains to obtain the relations given in Theorem
1.1
(after omitting the
s_{1,n}
generator) from the 2-skeleton of the Hassett space. The
1
-cells in the Hassett space contain pairs of endpoints of the form
\{x_{0},w_{p,q}x_{0}\}
for some 0-cell
x_{0}
, where either
p=q+1
or
a<q-p+1<n
. Since
w_{p,q}
is an involution, we may unambiguously label the
1
-cell with a formal symbol
s_{p,q}
, and we have
s_{p,q}^{2}=1
in
\tilde{J}_{n}^{a}
.

The remaining relations come from the boundaries of the
2
-cells adjacent to the base point. It is straightforward to verify that the 2-cells of types (
2
), (
3
), and (
4
) yield the commuting relations
s_{p,q}s_{m,r}=s_{m,r}s_{p,q}
and the cactus relations
s_{p,q}s_{m,r}=s_{p+q-r,p+q-m}s_{p,q}
. Finally, those of type (
1
) yield the braid relations
(s_{i,i+1}s_{i+1,i+2})^{3}=1
. (See Figure
12
.) Theorem
1.1
now follows from Proposition
2.7
.
∎

Remark 17
.

If
a=1
, then all the
2
-cells are of type (
4
); and if
a=2
, then the cells are all of types (
2
), (
3
), and (
4
). In either case, we recover the presentations of
J_{n}
and
\tilde{J}_{n}
.

4.
Generalized braid relations

As discussed in the introduction, the reduction map

\overline{M_{0,{n+1}}}(\mathbb{R})\twoheadrightarrow\overline{M_{0,{\mathcal{A}}}}(\mathbb{R})

induces a surjection
J_{n}\twoheadrightarrow J_{n}^{a}
of
S_{n}
-equivariant fundamental groups; see Equation (
3
). We conclude with a brief discussion of the kernel of this map. In particular, when
a\geq 3
, Theorem
1.1
gives a presentation of
J_{n}^{a}
using a subset of the generators of
J_{n}
, namely the
s_{p,q}
for which
q-p=1
or
q-p\in\{a,a+1,\ldots,n-1\}
. The remaining generators yield certain words in the kernel of the quotient map, which we refer to as the
generalized braid relations
. It is interesting to observe that these relations are, themselves, straightforward to establish by a geometric argument, essentially because the
p
-th through
q
-th marked points can collide without bubbling.

Lemma 4.1
.

Assume
a\geq 3
. Write
\sigma_{i}=s_{i,i+1}
and
s_{i,i}=1
. For
2\leq q-p\leq a-1
, we have the
generalized braid relations

s_{p,q}s_{p,q-1}\sigma_{q-1}\ldots\sigma_{p},\quad s_{p,q}\sigma_{q-1}\ldots\sigma_{p}s_{p+1,q}\in\ker(J_{n}\twoheadrightarrow J_{n}^{a}).

When
q=p+2
, the generalized braid relations reduce to the classical braid relations,

\sigma_{p}\sigma_{p+1}\sigma_{p}=\sigma_{p+1}\sigma_{p}\sigma_{p+1},

upon left cancellation of
s_{p,p+2}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha=s_{p,q}s_{p,q-1}\sigma_{q-1}\ldots\sigma_{p}
and
\beta=s_{p,q}\sigma_{q-1}\ldots\sigma_{p}s_{p+1,q}
. It is elementary to check that
\alpha,\beta
both map to
(1)\in S_{n}
, and so correspond to loops in
\overline{M_{0,\mathcal{A}}}(\mathbb{R})
. It remains to show that the loops
\alpha
and
\beta
are contractible.

Fix the marked points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{p}
,
x_{q+1},\ldots,x_{n+1}
at distinct locations on
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
. By
a
-stability, the map
\iota:\mathbb{A}^{q-p}(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow\overline{M_{0,\mathcal{A}}}(\mathbb{R})
given by
(x_{p+1},\ldots,x_{q})\mapsto(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1};x_{\bullet})
is well-defined and an embedding. Up to homotopy, for
p\leq m<r\leq q
,
s_{m,r}
represents a path in the Hassett space that lies completely in the image of
\iota
, regardless of the fixed starting point in this image. Thus,
\alpha
and
\beta
represent loops which lie completely in the image of
\iota
. Since
\mathbb{A}^{q-p}(\mathbb{R})
is simply connected, the loops
\alpha
and
\beta
are contractible.
∎

In other words, it is straightforward to show that the weighted cactus group
J_{n}^{a}
, defined abstractly as in Theorem
1.1
, surjects onto
\pi_{1}^{S_{n}}(\overline{M_{0,{\mathcal{A}({a})}}}(\mathbb{R}))
. We do not, however, know of a simple proof that this map is injective, i.e. that these relations generate the full kernel as a normal subgroup, without the construction of a cell decomposition.

We illustrate the effect of the reduction map in dimension
2
in Figure
12
, with the aid of our cell decomposition. In general,
s_{p,q}
reduces to a path across the center of a permutahedron of dimension
q-p
.

Figure 12
.
A local picture of the blowdown map
\overline{M_{0,{5}}}(\mathbb{R})\twoheadrightarrow\overline{M_{0,{\mathcal{A}({3})}}}(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Left: The central circle is an
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
with antipodal points identified, surrounded by three regions that are topological squares. Right: Merging regions and contracting the exceptional
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{1}
gives a hexagon
\Pi_{3}
, in which the path
s_{1,3}
becomes homotopic to
s_{1,2}s_{2,3}s_{1,2}
.
